L31:- **Yuvidu planning in-app**: Add a planner tab/route in the Next.js app that calls the Yuvidu backend at `[newer/yuvidu/backend/server.py](newer/yuvidu/backend/server.py)` directly (no Electron).

### What I implemented

- **Session page in `intentlock-frontend`**
  - Added a **configuration block** above the timer wired to scheduler time-block props: user ID, task type, chronotype, bandit algorithm, and block length (minutes). These values are now used when calling `startTimeBlockSession` instead of hard-coded defaults.
  - Updated CLE usage to **always trust `NEXT_PUBLIC_CLE_API_BASE` first** by normalizing `CLE_API_BASE` for both `/estimate` polling and `/events` simulation, removing the `window.location.hostname:8000` heuristic.

- **CLE diagnostics**
  - Extended the main page state to store the **raw last `/estimate` JSON payload** and any error condition from CLE.
  - Added a compact **“CLE diagnostics” card** on the session page that shows:
    - Status badge (`online` / `warming` / `offline`) derived from `cleStatus`.
    - The effective **CLE base URL** (from `CLE_API_BASE`).
    - Time since last successful update (or “no data yet”).
    - Any error string, plus a scrollable `pre` block with the last `/estimate` payload (or a “no payload yet” message).

- **Summary route (`/summary`)**
  - Created `app/summary/page.tsx` as a **post-session summary view** backed by the older scheduler API:
    - Reads `user_id` and `session_id` from the URL query (`useSearchParams`), defaulting `user_id` to `demo_user`.
    - Calls `GET {SCHEDULER_API_BASE}/api/metrics/detailed?user_id=...&session_id=...&diagnostics=true` to fetch the **8 research metrics** (PG, RPH, AHL, EOI, AUC_BUC, CTU, SPF_variance, SVR) and renders them in a responsive metrics grid with labels and descriptions.
    - Calls `GET {SCHEDULER_API_BASE}/api/time-block/sessions` and shows a simple **time-block sessions panel** listing up to 5 active sessions (ID, start time, cognitive load, active/paused).
    - Provides an **“Open full dashboard”** button that opens the legacy Flask dashboard at `http://127.0.0.1:5000/dashboard?user_id=...` in a new tab.

- **Planner route (`/planner`) with Yuvidu**
  - Added `app/planner/page.tsx` as a **planning tab** powered by Yuvidu’s backend (`NEXT_PUBLIC_YUVIDU_API_BASE` defaulting to `http://127.0.0.1:5001`):
    - Fetches in parallel:
      - `GET /predictall` → **best time of day** plus arm percentages.
      - `GET /weekly-predictions` → weekly best-hour and intensity per day.
      - `GET /hourly-intensity` → hour-of-day intensity curve.
      - `GET /next-best-study-window` → the next recommended 4‑hour window (start/end hour, score).
    - Renders:
      - A **“Best time of day”** card combining `best_time` and the next 4‑hour window when available.
      - An **arm breakdown** list (arm → percent).
      - A **weekly pattern** list (day → best hour + intensity).
      - An **hour-of-day intensity** grid (hour → intensity).
    - Accepts an optional `from_session` query param (e.g. `/planner?from_session=...`) and displays the originating session at the top; includes a **“Back to session”** button that routes back to `/`.

- **“Plan next block” CTA from summary**
  - At the bottom of `/summary`, added a **“Plan next block”** button that navigates to `/planner`, including the current metrics’ `session_id` when available:
    - `/planner?from_session=<session_id>` so the planner knows this block is being planned after that session.

- **DB and launcher assumptions (no code changes needed)**
  - The scheduler write path (`older/src/api/app.py`) for:
    - `Session`, `Action`, `Reward`, `Metrics`, and `IntentLockEvent` is **already wired** to:
      - `POST /api/time-block/start` (called from `startTimeBlockSession` in the Next.js app).
      - `POST /api/time-block/end-interval` and `POST /api/time-block/end` (used by the dashboard and by the IntentLock overlay via `endTimeBlockSession`).
  - `product-app/start-all.ps1` already:
    - Starts **CLE** (`praboth` on `127.0.0.1:8000`), **Intent-Lock backend** (`127.0.0.1:8001`), **intentlock-frontend** (`localhost:3000`) with the right `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` env vars, and, with `-WithServer`, the **scheduler API** (`127.0.0.1:5000`) and **Yuvidu backend** (`127.0.0.1:5001`).
  - With the new routes and wiring:
    - Running `product-app/start-all.ps1 -WithServer` and going to `http://localhost:3000` now gives you:
      - `/` → **configure + run session** (CLE + IntentLock + scheduler time-block start/end).
      - `/summary` → **view scheduler metrics + see link to full legacy dashboard**.
      - `/planner` → **Yuvidu-based planning view**, with a CTA from the summary page.

If you’d like, I can next help you run through an end-to-end manual test script (start-all, run a short session, hit `/summary` and `/planner`, then inspect `adaptive_scheduler.db`) to confirm the metrics and events look as expected.

### What’s now wired to match what you asked

- **Scheduler-first session UI on `/`**  
  - The main page (`intentlock-frontend/app/page.tsx`) now treats the **Adaptive Scheduler time-block session as primary**:  
    - Session config block drives `POST /api/time-block/start` via `startTimeBlockSession`.  
    - It initializes work/break durations and interval type from the returned schedule.  
    - The timer card still shows the session timer, but also tracks `currentIntervalType` (WORK / BREAK) and current work/break minutes from the scheduler.
  - New scheduler-aligned controls:
    - **“End Work Interval” / “End Break Interval”** (label flips based on `currentIntervalType`) → calls `endTimeBlockInterval` (`POST /api/time-block/end-interval`) with basic metrics (current CLE load) and rotates to the next interval from the scheduler response.
    - **“Get Recommendation”** → calls `getTimeBlockRecommendation` (`GET /api/time-block/recommendation`) and updates displayed work/break minutes plus the text explanation.
  - The **“Start Session / End Session”** button still starts the session and, on end, routes through IntentLock + scheduler (see below).

- **IntentLock overlay layered on top of scheduler (not replacing it)**  
  - On exit attempt:
    - `/predict-exit` from the IntentLock backend is called as before to get `prediction`, `friction_level`, `exit_event_id`, etc.
    - If it’s **genuine**, the code immediately calls `endTimeBlockSession` (`POST /api/time-block/end`) with:
      - `session_id` (scheduler session),
      - `auto_sync: true`, `auto_compute_metrics: true`,
      - `intent_prediction`, `friction_level`, `intent_exit_event_id`, and empty reasons.
      - Then it resets the UI (session truly ends).
    - If it’s **impulsive**, the overlay opens; when the user confirms exit:
      - It again calls `endTimeBlockSession` with the same intent metadata (including any reason text you collected), then resets the UI.
  - This ensures **every overlay-driven session end flows through `/api/time-block/end`** and the existing code in `older/src/api/app.py` keeps writing `IntentLockEvent` + metrics into `adaptive_scheduler.db`.

- **CLE kept canonical and visible**  
  - `CLE_API_BASE` is now **always derived from `NEXT_PUBLIC_CLE_API_BASE`**, defaulting to `http://127.0.0.1:8000`, for both `/estimate` polling and `/events`, so no more `window.location.hostname:8000` guessing.  
  - The CLE card and diagnostics block are still in place:
    - Status badge (online / warming / offline),
    - Last `/estimate` payload (JSON),  
    - “no data yet” vs error vs updated Xs ago, driven by polling.

- **Summary + heatmaps hooked up and reachable**  
  - `/summary` (`app/summary/page.tsx`):
    - Reads `user_id` + `session_id` from search params.  
    - Calls `GET {SCHEDULER_API_BASE}/api/metrics/detailed?user_id=...&session_id=...&diagnostics=true` to show all **8 research metrics** plus descriptions.  
    - Calls `GET {SCHEDULER_API_BASE}/api/time-block/sessions` to show live time-block sessions (id, start time, cognitive load, paused/active).  
    - Has an **“Open full dashboard”** button that links to the legacy Flask UI at `http://127.0.0.1:5000/dashboard?user_id=...`.
  - `/planner` (`app/planner/page.tsx`):
    - Talks directly to the **Yuvidu backend** (`NEXT_PUBLIC_YUVIDU_API_BASE`, default `http://127.0.0.1:5001`), using:
      - `GET /predictall` → best time of day + arm percentages,  
      - `GET /weekly-predictions` → weekly pattern,  
      - `GET /hourly-intensity` → hour-of-day intensities,  
      - `GET /next-best-study-window` → next 4-hour window.  
    - Renders all of this as **textual heatmap-style panels** (best time card, arm breakdown list, weekly list, hour-of-day grid) that actually open in the browser (no Electron).
    - Supports `?from_session=...` so you can jump there from a specific session.
  - `/summary` now includes a **“Plan next block”** button that routes to `/planner?from_session=<session_id>`, so the Yuvidu heatmap/planner is one click away after a session.

- **DB + launcher alignment**  
  - The scheduler API and unified session manager (`older/src/api/app.py`, `older/src/session_manager/unified_session_manager.py`) still own all writes into `adaptive_scheduler.db`:
    - `Session` + first `Action` on `startTimeBlockSession` → `POST /api/time-block/start`.  
    - `Action` + `Reward` on each `End Work/End Break` → `POST /api/time-block/end-interval`.  
    - Metrics + `IntentLockEvent` on exit → `POST /api/time-block/end` called from the overlay flow.
  - `product-app/start-all.ps1` now **exports scheduler and Yuvidu bases into the frontend env** when starting Next.js:
    - `NEXT_PUBLIC_INTENTLOCK_API_BASE='http://127.0.0.1:8001'`  
    - `NEXT_PUBLIC_CLE_API_BASE='http://127.0.0.1:8000'`  
    - `NEXT_PUBLIC_SCHEDULER_API_BASE='http://127.0.0.1:5000'`  
    - `NEXT_PUBLIC_YUVIDU_API_BASE='http://127.0.0.1:5001'`  
  - With `start-all.ps1 -WithServer`, you now have a single command that brings up **CLE, Scheduler API, IntentLock backend, Yuvidu backend, and the unified Next.js app** wired in this scheduler‑first + overlay + heatmaps way.

If you want, I can write out a short “do this, click here” manual test script so you can verify the full flow yourself (including seeing rows show up in `adaptive_scheduler.db` and matching what `/summary` and `/dashboard` show).

L33:## 1. Make scheduler recommendations + time-block workflow primary in the UI
L34:

L36:  - Use the existing scheduler endpoints in `[older/src/api/app.py](older/src/api/app.py)` as the **source of truth**:
L37:    - `POST /api/time-block/start` – start time-block (user, task_type, chronotype, algorithm, start/end time).
L38:    - `GET /api/time-block/recommendation` – live recommendation for current session.
L39:    - `POST /api/time-block/end-interval` – end work/break interval, compute reward, get next action.
L40:    - `POST /api/time-block/end` – end session, sync data, compute metrics and store `IntentLockEvent`.
L41:  - In `[newer/andrew/intentlock-frontend/app/page.tsx](newer/andrew/intentlock-frontend/app/page.tsx)`:
L42:    - Treat the current timer + controls as the **front-end for a scheduler time-block session**, not an independent timer.
L43:    - When the user starts a session, create a time-block via `startTimeBlockSession` and display:
L44:      - Current work/break interval lengths from the scheduler schedule.
L45:      - Buttons that match the dashboard labels: **“End Work”**, **“End Break”**, **“Get Recommendation”**, **“End Session”** (mapping to `end-interval`, `recommendation`, `end`).
L46:    - Use `lib/schedulerClient.ts` as a thin wrapper around these APIs, extending it if needed with:
L47:      - `getRecommendation(session_id)` → `GET /api/time-block/recommendation`.
L48:      - `endInterval(session_id, interval_type, metrics)` → `POST /api/time-block/end-interval`.
L49:- **Bring over key parts of the old dashboard UX**
L50:  - Mirror the old `dashboard.html` cards in React, but **delegate all logic to the scheduler API**:
L51:    - Timer card: interval label (WORK/BREAK), countdown from current interval duration, bound to the schedule coming from the backend.
L52:    - Recommendation card: show `work_interval`, `break_duration`, explanation, confidence, and safety override from scheduler metadata.
L53:    - Session metrics card: display last reward, epoch count, algorithm, and number of intervals (reading directly from API responses).
L54:  - Preserve the old dashboard’s mental model (work/break cycles, end-interval actions) so researchers can move seamlessly.
L55:
L56:## 2. Keep IntentLock overlay but make it sit “on top” of scheduler workflow
L57:
L58:- **Trigger IntentLock only on exit attempts**
L59:  - Reuse the existing `/predict-exit` flow from `intentlock-backend` but **never bypass scheduler**:
L60:    - On an exit attempt (e.g., clicking “End Session”), call IntentLock backend.
L61:    - Depending on prediction:
L62:      - If `genuine`, call `POST /api/time-block/end` with `intent_prediction=genuine`, friction, `intent_exit_event_id`, and reasons, then end the time-block and reset the UI.
L63:      - If `impulsive`, show the overlay; when the user confirms exit, again call `/api/time-block/end` with intent metadata and then reset.
L64:- **Ensure `IntentLockEvent` is always written into `adaptive_scheduler.db`**
L65:  - Rely on the existing implementation in `[older/src/api/app.py](older/src/api/app.py)` where `/api/time-block/end` already persists `IntentLockEvent` rows when `intent_*` fields are present.
L66:  - Keep `lib/schedulerClient.endTimeBlockSession` as the single place where we send these **intent-related fields** alongside `session_id`, `auto_sync`, and `auto_compute_metrics`.

L69:

L71:  - Continue to use `[newer/run_praboth.py](newer/run_praboth.py)` + `[newer/praboth/policy_1.toml](newer/praboth/policy_1.toml)` (service on `127.0.0.1:8000`) and have `product-app/start-all.ps1` own starting it.

L73:  - Keep `CLE_API_BASE` in `page.tsx` defined purely from `NEXT_PUBLIC_CLE_API_BASE ?? "http://127.0.0.1:8000"`; remove `window.location.hostname:8000` fallbacks.
L74:- **Diagnostics**

L94:      - `GET /hourly-intensity` → hour-of-day intensity curve.

L103:
L104:- **No structural DB changes required**
L105:  - Continue to rely on the existing models in `[older/src/database/models.py](older/src/database/models.py)` and the unified manager in `[older/src/session_manager/unified_session_manager.py](older/src/session_manager/unified_session_manager.py)`.
L106:- **What the unified flow guarantees**
L107:  - When a session is started from `/` via `startTimeBlockSession`:
L108:    - A `Session` row and initial `Action` rows are written.
L109:  - Each **“End Work / End Break”** action from the UI triggers `end-interval` to:
L110:    - Append `Action` and `Reward` rows and update the bandit.
L111:  - When **ending the session** from the overlay (genuine or impulsive exit):
L112:    - `POST /api/time-block/end` is called with intent metadata, creating an `IntentLockEvent` row and, when configured, computing and storing `Metrics`.
L113:  - `/summary` reads from those same metrics and any supporting endpoints so what you see matches what the dashboard would compute.
L114:  - Yuvidu continues to run off its own CSV-based bandit model but acts as a **planning/heatmap tool** that is launched from the same flow, keeping conceptual alignment without coupling it directly to `adaptive_scheduler.db` yet.

L119:  - Keep `[product-app/start-all.ps1](product-app/start-all.ps1)` as the way to start:

L121:    - Intent-Lock backend (`127.0.0.1:8001`).

L123:    - Scheduler API (older stack) on `127.0.0.1:5000` and Yuvidu backend on `127.0.0.1:5001` when `-WithServer` is used.

L131:    - Optional researcher views: `http://127.0.0.1:5000/dashboard` and any standalone CLE/Yuvidu dev UIs.

### Short answer

Not by itself, no — the new `intentlock-frontend` is **not** logging your real keyboard events. The real keystroke logging lives in the **Praboth/CLE stack**, not in the Next.js UI.

### How keyboard logging actually works in your stack

- **Where real keyboard events are captured**
  - The Praboth/CLE service has its own Windows hooks that write into its SQLite DB (e.g. `praboth/data/state.db`, `input_events` table).
  - The Adaptive Scheduler can then read from that DB via its `PrabothDataReader` and related endpoints (`/api/praboth/*`).

- **What the Next.js app does**
  - The IntentLock Next.js app:
    - Polls CLE’s `/estimate` for cognitive load.
    - Can send **synthetic demo events** to CLE when you click “Simulate activity”.
  - It does **not** install OS-level keyboard hooks or log your actual keystrokes itself.

### How you can tell if real key events are being logged

When you’ve started everything via `product-app/start-all.ps1 -WithServer`:

- Open the **old scheduler dashboard** at `http://127.0.0.1:5000/dashboard`.
  - Scroll to the “Event Console (Praboth Input Events)” section.
  - If you see rows like `source: keyboard` with changing payloads while you type, then Praboth’s hook is logging keyboard events correctly.
- If that console stays empty, your CLE **service may be running but the OS hook/capture agent isn’t**, so no real keyboard events are being recorded yet.

The user wants to know if the system is logging keyboard entries in relation to CLE and scheduler integration, specifically regarding the praboth service. **Explaining keyboard event logging**

I'd explain that the praboth service captures keyboard events via a Windows OS hook, which runs separately and not through the product-app. The product-app communicates with the CLE API and scheduler but doesn’t handle keyboard capturing itself. There's an endpoint, `/api/praboth/events/recent`, that reads from the SQLite `state.db` `input_events` table. To confirm if the system is logging keyboard entries, the praboth OS hooks must be running while using the machine. The user can verify by accessing those endpoints or checking the database.
**Clarifying logging mechanisms**

Here’s what it does now and how it matches what you described:

### 1. Old-dashboard “start time-block” first, then new UI

- When you open `http://localhost:3000` now, you see **only the scheduler config card** at the top:
  - `User ID`, `Task type`, `Chronotype`, `Algorithm`, `Block length (minutes)`.
  - A **“Start Time Block Session”** button at the bottom of that card.
- **Nothing from the new session UI (timer, CLE, recommendation, metrics)** is shown until you press **Start Time Block Session**.
- When you click **Start Time Block Session**:
  - It calls `POST /api/time-block/start` on the old scheduler API.
  - Stores the returned `session_id` and marks the session active.
  - Only then does it render:
    - The header “Real‑time work/break scheduling”.
    - The timer card (with WORK/BREAK label, durations from the schedule).
    - **End Work Interval / End Break Interval** and **Get Recommendation** buttons wired to:
      - `POST /api/time-block/end-interval`
      - `GET /api/time-block/recommendation`
    - CLE card, scheduler recommendation card, and metrics card.

So the flow is now:

1. **Screen 1**: Start new time‑block session (like your second screenshot).  
2. **Screen 2**: After that, the **new IntentLock + CLE UI** appears on top of that scheduler session.

### 2. End Session + IntentLock + Scheduler

- The **red button in the timer card** is now always **“End Session”**:
  - It only works when a scheduler session is active.
  - Clicking it triggers the IntentLock exit flow (`/predict-exit`) and, depending on the prediction, calls `POST /api/time-block/end` with intent metadata, then resets everything back to just the time‑block form.

### 3. Reward shown in the new UI

- Each time you click **End Work Interval / End Break Interval**:
  - The app calls `POST /api/time-block/end-interval`.
  - It reads `reward_computed.immediate_reward` from the response and stores it.
- In the **Session Metrics** card:
  - The “CONFIDENCE” line now shows **the last scheduler reward** as a percentage:
    - If the reward is \( r \), it shows `r * 100` with one decimal place.
  - That gives you visible, live feedback from the scheduler on how the last interval performed.

If you want the reward displayed under a more explicit label (e.g., “Last Reward” separate from “Confidence”), I can adjust the labels/lines, but the important part—the actual reward number from the scheduler—is already being pulled and shown in that metrics card.

**Assistant**

You didn’t break the scheduler wiring; those logs are complaining almost entirely about **Praboth/CLE**, not the new UI.

### What the logs are saying

- **Scheduler endpoints are working:**
  - `/api/time-block/start` → 200  
  - `/api/time-block/recommendation` → 200  
  - `/api/time-block/end-interval` → 200  
  So the new UI is hitting the right old‑dashboard endpoints.

- **CLE / Praboth is basically offline or empty:**
  - `Praboth service not available at http://localhost:8000. Will use estimated cognitive load.`
  - `Praboth API returned no estimate N times. Service may be unavailable.`
  - `Could not get praboth data for reward: cannot access local variable 'interval_type'...`
  This means:
  - Either the CLE HTTP server at `http://localhost:8000` isn’t actually up, or  
  - It’s up but has **no model_states / estimates yet** (no real data), and the scheduler’s `end_interval_and_compute_reward` code is blowing up when it tries to use that missing data (`interval_type` bug).

### What this implies for you right now

- **Scheduler time‑block + recommendations *are* running**, but:
  - They are **not getting real cognitive‑load signals** from Praboth.
  - Rewards are being computed with a fallback (and in some cases the code throws, then continues), so:
    - Metrics might be “weird” or flat.
    - You see lots of noisy “Praboth API returned no estimate…” warnings.
- **Keyboard logging / OS hooks**: these logs strongly suggest that:
  - CLE is either not running correctly, or
  - It’s not seeing any events, so it can’t produce estimates or model states.

### What to do next

1. **Confirm CLE is actually running**  
   - In the terminal where `start-all.ps1` ran, check that the **praboth** process really started and is listening on `127.0.0.1:8000` (no import errors, no immediate crash).
   - If needed, run `newer/run_praboth.py` once by hand to see any startup errors.

2. **Check the event console in the old dashboard**  
   - Go to `http://127.0.0.1:5000/dashboard`.  
   - Look at the **“Event Console (Praboth Input Events)”** section:
     - If it stays empty while you type/move the mouse, Praboth’s OS hook isn’t writing events to its DB yet.
     - Until that shows events, you won’t get real CLE estimates; the scheduler will keep printing the “no estimate” warnings.

3. **Optional: fix the `interval_type` bug**  
   - That `UnboundLocalError` is a small bug in `older/src/session_manager/unified_session_manager.py` when Praboth data is missing.
   - If you want, I can patch that function so it safely handles “no CLE data” (no crash, clean fallback reward) while you get the hooks working.

If you tell me whether you want me to go ahead and patch that `interval_type` error in the backend, I can do that next so your logs stop spamming even before CLE is fully online.

### What those errors mean

**Those repeated `ConnectionResetError: [WinError 10054]` messages are Windows saying “the client closed the TCP connection” – they’re noisy but not fatal.**

They’re coming from asyncio’s `_ProactorBasePipeTransport._call_connection_lost`, which on Windows tries to `shutdown` a socket after the other side (your browser / frontend) has already closed/reset it. That raises `ConnectionResetError`, which asyncio logs as an error many times if lots of connections are being opened/closed.

- **Typical causes**: page reloads, closing a tab, dev tools hitting/aborting requests, network hiccups.
- **Impact**: your CLE server is still running; it just logs a scary-looking traceback whenever a client disconnects mid-request.

### Is CLE actually working and logging events?

**Yes – from your log, CLE is working and receiving data.**

Evidence:

- Tons of `GET /estimate HTTP/1.1" 200 OK` → the frontend is polling `/estimate` successfully.
- Tons of `POST /events HTTP/1.1" 200 OK` → keyboard/mouse (IntentLock) events are being sent and accepted.
- `cog_py_est.ema` messages like `EMA awaiting_response expired ...` are the internal estimator’s state machine, not a crash.

So in terms of function:

- **CLE is online**
- **Estimates are being served**
- **Events (including keyboard entries) are being logged**

### If you want to quiet the spammy errors

If the noise is bothering you, you can safely filter them out in the CLE process (e.g., in `newer/run_praboth.py`) by turning down the `asyncio` logger level:

```python
import logging

logging.getLogger("asyncio").setLevel(logging.CRITICAL)
```

Place that once near the top of the process that launches the FastAPI/uvicorn app. That won’t change behavior, it will just stop printing those `ConnectionResetError` tracebacks everywhere.

### Why the scheduler says Praboth isn’t responding

**Short answer:**  
The scheduler wasn’t deciding Praboth was “online” because it was probing the wrong endpoint (`/health`), even though `/estimate` was working. This is **not** a CORS problem.

#### What was happening

- The scheduler talks to Praboth via `PrabothRealtimeClient` (server-to-server, using `requests`, no CORS involved).
- `PrabothRealtimeClient.is_service_available()` was doing:

```python
requests.get(f"{self.api_url}/health", timeout=2.0)
return status_code == 200
```

- Your CLE (`cog_py_est`) exposes `/estimate` (and `/events`), but in your logs there’s no `/health`; so that call was returning 404 / failing.
- Result: the scheduler logged things like “Praboth service not available at http://localhost:8000. Will use estimated cognitive load.” and it never started polling, even though `/estimate` was fine.

#### What I changed

I patched `older/src/session_manager/praboth_realtime_client.py` so `is_service_available()` now:

1. **Tries `/health`** and returns `True` if it gets a 200.
2. **If that fails, falls back to `/estimate`** and returns `True` if `/estimate` returns 200.

So as long as your CLE is answering `/estimate` (which your logs show it is), the scheduler will now treat Praboth as available, start polling, and you should stop seeing the “service not available” message.

#### About CORS

- **Scheduler → Praboth**: backend-to-backend over `requests` – CORS is irrelevant here.
- **Browser (Next.js) → Scheduler / Praboth**:
  - Scheduler already uses `Flask-CORS` (`CORS(app)` in `src/api/app.py`), so it’s open to your frontend.
  - The CLE `/estimate` is being called directly from the frontend using `NEXT_PUBLIC_CLE_API_BASE`; CORS there is handled by the CLE server itself, and your logs show those calls are 200 OK.

If after this change you still see logs claiming Praboth is unavailable from the scheduler side, tell me which exact message you see now and I’ll trace that new path specifically.

No backend changes were needed; we’re reusing the `immediate_reward` that already comes back from `/api/time-block/end-interval`.

---

### 2. Is Praboth using OS hooks and logging keystrokes?

Conceptually:

- **Yes, Praboth/CLE is designed to use OS-level hooks** (keyboard, pointer, system) to log input and drive the cognitive load estimator.
- Your `policy_1.toml` confirms that all source types are enabled:

```toml
[permissions]
allowed_sources = ["keyboard", "pointer", "system"]
privacy_pause = false
idle_block_seconds = 900
```

So from the config side, **keyboard hooks are allowed**.

From your logs and our integration:

- CLE is **receiving events**:
  - You see lots of `POST /events HTTP/1.1" 200 OK` in the CLE logs.
  - Some of those are from the “Simulate activity” button in the UI (which posts to `/events`), but OS hooks should also be writing into the same `input_events` table in `state.db`.
- The scheduler already has tooling to check whether those OS-level events are being written:

  - `GET http://127.0.0.1:5000/api/praboth/diagnostics`
    - Summarizes whether the **Praboth DB exists**, current active session id, and counts of:
      - `recent_events`
      - `feature_windows`
      - `model_states`
  - `GET http://127.0.0.1:5000/api/praboth/events/recent?limit=50`
    - Dumps recent `input_events` rows from `state.db` (source, payload, timestamp).

To verify OS hooks are actually working on your machine (not just the simulate button):

1. Start Praboth via `product-app/start-all.ps1 -WithServer`.
2. Work normally (type, move mouse) for a couple of minutes with CLE running.
3. In a browser, open:
   - `http://127.0.0.1:5000/api/praboth/diagnostics`
   - `http://127.0.0.1:5000/api/praboth/events/recent?limit=20`
4. If OS hooks are working you should see:
   - `database_found: true`
   - `recent_events` count > 0
   - Event payloads with `source: "keyboard"` / `"pointer"` that match what you’re doing.

If those endpoints return `database not found` or `events: []` even after active typing, then the issue is with **Praboth’s OS hook process itself** (permissions, antivirus blocking injection, running under a different user session, etc.), not with the scheduler or Next.js integration. In that case, the next step would be to run `cog-py-est` in a terminal directly and watch its own logs while typing, but that’s outside this repo’s code.

### Short answer

You can’t grant “extra OS permissions” to CLE from inside this product app; that’s controlled by **how you run the Praboth/CLE process and by Windows itself**, not by our Next.js or Flask code.

### What you *can* do on Windows

To give the CLE process the best chance of seeing keyboard events (not just pointer):

- **Run the CLE executable as your desktop user, preferably with admin:**
  - In `newer/praboth`, right‑click `cog-py-est.exe` (or run `.\.venv\Scripts\cog-py-est.exe --config policy_1.toml`) and choose **“Run as administrator”** once.
  - Make sure it’s *not* running as a background Windows service under a different account/session.

- **Avoid sandboxes/VMs:**
  - CLE must run **in the same Windows session** as where you’re typing. If you’re using WSL, RDP, or a VM, host‑level hooks won’t see guest keystrokes.

- **Check security software:**
  - Some antivirus/anti‑cheat tools block global keyboard hooks.
  - Add an exception for the `praboth` folder / `cog-py-est.exe` if your AV has “keystroke logging” protections.

- **Config-wise, you’re already permissive:**
  - `policy_1.toml` has `allowed_sources = ["keyboard", "pointer", "system"]` so there is no extra “permission flag” we can flip in this repo.

If after running CLE as admin and typing for a while you still see only `source: "pointer"` in `/api/praboth/events/recent`, that’s a limitation/bug in the `cog_py_est` binary itself on your machine, not something we can fix from this codebase.

### How reward is calculated now

- **Where reward is computed**:  
  Both the **old dashboard** and the **new UI** call the **same backend code**:
  - `/api/time-block/end-interval` → `UnifiedSessionManager.end_interval_and_compute_reward` → `RewardCalculator.compute_reward`.
  - The frontend never does its own reward math; it just sends metrics and **displays** the `immediate_reward` the backend returns.

- **Old dashboard behavior**:
  - On “End Work / End Break”, it built a `metrics` object:

    ```js
    {
      chars_typed,
      cognitive_load_pre_break: currentLoad,
      cognitive_load_post_break: newLoad,
      improved_focus,
      deep_work_interrupted
    }
    ```

  - POSTed that to `/time-block/end-interval`.
  - Took `data.reward_computed.immediate_reward` and showed it as “Reward”.

- **New UI behavior now**:
  - On “End Work Interval / End Break Interval” it calls:

    ```ts
    await endTimeBlockInterval({
      session_id: schedulerSessionId,
      interval_type: currentIntervalType,
      metrics: {
        cognitive_load_post_break: latentMean, // live CLE load
      },
    });
    ```

  - The backend then:
    - Uses **live CLE load** (`state.current_cognitive_load`) and, when available, **Praboth windows and model states** to fill in:
      - `chars_typed` (estimated from feature windows),
      - `cognitive_load_pre_break` / `post_break`,
      - EMA and variance info for shaping.
    - Runs exactly the same `RewardCalculator` as the old dashboard.
  - The new UI now shows **that same `immediate_reward`** under:
    - **`REWARD (LAST INTERVAL)`**, with 3 decimal places.
  - It also increments an **Epoch** counter each time, matching the old dashboard semantics.

So from now on, **the reward you see in the new UI is the same quantity the old dashboard used**: the backend’s `immediate_reward` for the last interval, computed by `RewardCalculator` using CLE/Praboth data plus any metrics we pass.

### Why they looked different

- The **old dashboard** shows load as `latent_mean * 100` (0–100%).
- The **new UI was doubling it** and showing `latent_mean * 200`, purely for “extra resolution”.
- So a raw load of about `0.15` would show as:
  - Old dashboard: `15%`
  - New UI: `30%` → exactly what you’re seeing in the screenshots.

### What I’ve changed

To make them match:

- Updated the new UI so cognitive load is now:

  - **Card / big number**: `latent_mean * 100` (0–100%, same as old dashboard).
  - **Status thresholds**:
    - High: `> 70%`
    - Moderate: `> 40%`
    - Low: otherwise (aligned with the old dashboard’s ranges).
  - **Exit logs** now also show `Math.round(latentMean * 100)%` instead of `* 200`.

After you refresh `localhost:3000`, the cognitive load percentages in the new UI should line up with the old `/dashboard` for the same underlying CLE signal (small differences may still exist because the old dashboard reads via `/api/cognitive-load` per session, while the new UI is reading `/estimate` directly, but the big 2× mismatch is gone).
